When Types Intersect and Effects Get Handled
Stefano Catozi, Ugo Dal Lago, Taro Sekiyama

Abstract
. We introduce a novel intersection type system for a λ \lambda -calculus with algebraic effects and handlers. The system, inherently behavioral in nature, enjoys the classical properties of intersection type systems, in particular subject reduction and expansion . It thus characterizes the set of terms whose evaluation process terminates and, at the same time, allows reducing the reachability problem to type inference. This new system, the first with these features for a calculus with handlers, induces a system of simple types which, although not guaranteeing termination, is type sound and admits a decidable HOMC problem, unlike similar type systems like Dal Lago and Ghyselen’s \HEPCF .



1.
Introduction

Intersection types
, introduced by Coppo and Dezani almost fifty years
ago
(
11
)
, were initially developed as a powerful tool for the analysis of the
semantics and meta-theory of the

\lambda
-calculus. In particular, they made it possible to
characterize fundamental program properties such as termination (spelled out in different ways, like
strong normalization or head-normal-form normalization), and they
naturally led to the development of semantic models such as filter
models
(
3
;
5
)
. From this perspective,
their role was from the outset quite
different from that of traditional typing, which provides a verification mechanism for
safety properties via type checking and type inference, the main reason being
that in their most general form, intersection type theories give rise to

undecidable
type inference and type checking problems.
This distinguished aspect of intersection types enables applications which are
unattainable via simple types.

For example,
30
made the fundamental observation that the
decidability of the
higher-order model-checking
(HOMC)
(
26
;
27
;
38
;
32
)
problem can be
understood as a form of exhaustive type inference in a system of uniform
intersection types
refining
an underlying system of simple
types.
The key idea is that this refinement enjoys a
finite model property
: each
simple type admits only a finite number of refinements, and this finiteness
makes exhaustive search possible, ultimately guaranteeing the decidability of
the model checking. The aforementioned decidability results
for the HOMC problem apply to calculi in the style of Plotkin’s
\mathsf{PCF}
which, although equipped
with higher-order functions and recursion, are simply typed and only feature finite
base types. Relaxing either of these two conditions quickly leads to undecidability
(
29
)
.
As shown in numerous subsequent works, the decidability results mentioned above
are also fragile with respect to extensions of the underlying language with effects,
e.g. probabilistic choice
(
28
)
.
Particularly noteworthy is the extension of
\mathsf{PCF}
with
algebraic effects

(
40
)
and
handlers

(
41
)
, which have been the subject of
extensive study over the past twenty years, and which allow the task of providing an
implementation for algebraic operations to be delegated to the programmer by
reifying
delimited continuations
. In presence of effect handlers,
Dal Lago and Ghyselen
(
13
)
show that HOMC becomes undecidable.
It should be noted that although the HOMC problem is traditionally studied when the
underlying specification is
any MSO formula
,
reachability properties
are
sufficient to render the problem undecidable in presence of probabilistic choice or
effect handlers. In the rest of this paper we focus on reachability properties.

Intersection type systems have themselves been studied for extensions of the

\lambda
-calculus equipped with specific forms of
effects
, such as
nondeterministic choice
(
15
)
, probabilistic
choice
(
9
;
12
)
, and global
state
(
2
)
. More recently,
Gavazzo et al.
(
18
)
have undertaken the development of a generic framework
for defining intersection types for calculi with algebraic
effects, in which the monad interpreting the algebraic operations of
the calculus is internalized within the type system, thereby allowing the
behavior of terms to be reflected at the level of types.
But what if algebraic operations are not given meaning through monads, with their interpretation delegated to programmers?
Studying intersection type systems for effect handlers seems to be a natural approach
to the semantic analysis of higher-order programs handling effects by themselves.
Moreover, intersection types appear as a promising tool in the quest for
type systems for programs with effect handlers for which the HOMC problem remains decidable:
it would suffice to ensure that the finite model property remains true.
To the authors’ knowledge, however, there is still no account of intersection types in
calculi with effect handlers.

It is therefore natural to ask whether a system of intersection types can be
defined for calculi with handlers, and the aim of this article is precisely to
provide a first piece of positive evidence in this direction.
A key point to define an intersection type system enjoying the desired properties,
such as a characterization of the set of terminating terms, is to provide a type
discipline that can faithfully express programs’ behavior,
e.g.

functions are assigned an intersection type that express all the usages of the
functions’ arguments in their bodies.
In the presence of algebraic effects and handlers, the behavior of a program
relies on
what algebraic operation is called in what order
and
how their continuations are used by the underlying effect handler
.
To embody this, the paper introduces a new form of behavioural types that
captures the
computation tree
of algebraic operations produced by each
term, together with a specification of how the term itself expects the
environment to handle these operations.
This is fully in line with the spirit of intersection types, in that there is no
loss of information.

The resulting system enjoys the properties typically expected of an intersection type system. In particular, alongside the standard subject
reduction
property, it also satisfies a property known as subject
expansion
, which states that types are preserved backwards along reductions. This property, in turn, allows one to prove that the type system characterizes termination, in analogy with what happens in the
\lambda
-calculus. In addition to subject expansion, another non-trivial step in the proof of this result consists in showing that all typable terms are terminating, which requires an adaptation of Tait’s reducibility technique
(
55
)
to the introduced behavioral type system.

Remarkable as the above results are, they are not the only contributions of this work. We also present a study of intersection types viewed as refinements of simple types in the presence of effect handlers. This analysis provides a precise confirmation of the undecidability result of the HOMC problem for
\HEPCF
, a PCF calculus with algebraic effects and handlers
(
13
)
. We achieve this result by showing that when intersection types are regarded as refinements of simple types, the finite refinement property
fails
: there are simple types which can be refined in infinitely many distinct ways. However, this is not the end of the story. The very structure of behavioural types as described above suggests the existence of a novel type system, itself behavioral in nature, and for which, instead, the finite model property
does
hold, and consequently the HOMC problem is decidable. This is not the first fragment of
\HEPCF
where the HOMC problem is decidable
(
49
;
47
;
17
)
, but the methodology employed in all the previous approaches consists in defining
CPS transformations
from the calculus under consideration to a finitary PCF—a variant of PCF only with finite base types, where the HOMC problem is decidable
(
38
)
—and in proving that such transformations are adequate and type-preserving. However, it was still unclear what the deeper reason is behind the (un)decidability of the HOMC problem for
\HEPCF
. Our intersection type system provides semantic evidence on that: whether a finite model exists or not. Our work is thus a further witness that intersection types are indeed a powerful and flexible tool, not only in a semantic setting, but also in verification.

This paper’s contributions can be summarized as follows:

•

Introduction of
\mathsf{HEBI}
, an intersection type system for a calculus with algebraic effects and handlers. The system combines in a novel way the classical notion of intersection types—here taking the form of a
set
of types—with a form of behavioral typing in which the algebraic operations generated by a term are recorded within the underlying type. This is in the first part of Section
4
.

•

Proof of soundness and completeness of
\mathsf{HEBI}
with respect to termination and reachability. The proof relies on a forward and backward type preservation result (i.e., subject reduction and expansion), as well as on a reducibility argument generalizing Tait’s classical method to the behavioral types forming the core of
\mathsf{HEBI}
. This is in the second part of Section
4
.

•

Introduction of a simply typed variant of
\mathsf{HEBI}
, called
\mathsf{HEB}
, together with a proof that the reachability problem becomes decidable in the system. The proof is structured around the observation that
\mathsf{HEBI}
types can be regarded as refinements of
\mathsf{HEB}
types, and that this refinement enjoys the finite refinement property. All this can be found in Section
5
.

•

A negative result about the finite refinement property for
\HEPCF
, shedding some light on why the later has an
undecidable HOMC problem. This is in Section
6
.

In the rest of the paper, these contributions are presented after giving an overview (
Section

2
) and some preliminaries (
Section

3
) about
\HEPCF
and its untyped version.

2.
A High-Level View

In this section we aim to provide an informal account of our contribution, delving slightly more into the details while avoiding overly technical discussions.

First of all, let us try to understand in what sense intersection types can be seen as a way of steadily increasing the expressive power of simple types, while preserving their good properties. In the simply typed
\lambda
-calculus, as is well known, the term
(\lambda x.\lambda y.\langle x\;3,y\;\mathtt{false}\rangle)(\lambda z.z)(\lambda w.w)
is typable, whereas its close relative

(1)

(\lambda x.\langle x\;3,x\;\mathtt{false}\rangle)(\lambda z.z)

is
not
. This is due to the fact that simple types do not support any form of type
polymorphism
. Simple types can be extended with polymorphic features in at least two different ways. On the one hand, one may add
parametric polymorphism
in the style of that present in System
F

(
20
;
44
)
. In this setting, the variable
x
in (
1
) would be assigned the type
\forall\alpha.\alpha\rightarrow\alpha
. Alternatively, one may opt for a form of
ad-hoc
,
bounded
polymorphism, in which the same variable is instead assigned an
intersection type
of the form
(\mathit{int}\rightarrow\mathit{int})\cap(\mathit{bool}\rightarrow\mathit{bool})
, meaning that the variable may be used as (and requires the call site to give) a function of type
\mathit{int}\rightarrow\mathit{int}

and

\mathit{bool}\rightarrow\mathit{bool}
. From many perspectives, the former solution is preferable, as is well known. There is, however, a distinctive feature of intersection types that is not shared by parametric polymorphism, namely their ability to
characterize
terms satisfying interesting termination properties. Ultimately, this is due to the fact that a fundamental lemma—namely, the so called
anti-substitution
lemma (from which subject expansion follows)—holds naturally for intersection types, whereas it cannot hold in parametric polymorphism.

The anti-substitution lemma, in a form that we keep deliberately oversimplified, states that whenever there exists a derivation
\Pi
of a typing judgment
\vdash t\{x/v\}:A
, it is possible to extract from it a type
B
such that
x:B\vdash t:A
and
\vdash v:B
. Clearly, this is not possible in simple types nor even parametric polymorphism, whereas it becomes feasible in the presence of the intersection construct, which allows one to collect into
B

all the types
that
\Pi
assigns to the (possibly many) occurrences of
v
found within
t\{x/v\}
. In fact, parametric polymorphism cannot satisfy the anti-substitution lemma because not all pairs of distinct types can be unified into one, i.e., it is not possible to find one type
B
accounting for all possible types
\Pi
attributes to
v
, because polymorphism imposes a uniformity condition. If such a property held, parametrically polymorphic type systems would be complete for termination, and in fact they are not, see e.g.
(
54
)
for more detail and a counterexample.
As an example of how intersection types enable the anti-substitution property, consider a derivation
\Pi
of
\vdash\langle x\;3,x\;\mathtt{false}\rangle\{x/\lambda z.z\}:\mathit{int}\times\mathit{bool}
(where the subject term is the result of reducing (
1
)), the anti-substitution lemma guarantees that
x:(\mathit{int}\rightarrow\mathit{int})\cap(\mathit{bool}\rightarrow\mathit{bool})\vdash\langle x\;3,x\;\mathtt{false}\rangle:\mathit{int}\times\mathit{bool}
and
\vdash\lambda z.z:(\mathit{int}\rightarrow\mathit{int})\cap(\mathit{bool}\rightarrow\mathit{bool})
are also derivable, where the intersection type assigned to
x
and
\lambda z.z
collects all the types assigned to the occurrences of
\lambda z.z
in
\Pi
. This collecting operation is made possible by the liberal nature of ad-hoc polymorphism, and by the fact that the types assigned to the substituted value
v
can be completely uncorrelated
and
the resulting polymorphic type keeps track of
all
of them. Furthermore, this property leads to subject expansion, which states that, given terms
t
and
s
such that
t
reduces to
s
and
\vdash s:A
holds,
\vdash t:A
also holds. This is fundamentally different from what happens in parametric polymorphism, and guarantees that
any
terminating term is typechecked. For example, given a term
t
that terminates at a constant value, say,
0
, since
0
is well typed, the subject expansion guarantees that the term
t
is also well typed.
1
1

1

It is easy to generalize to terms that terminate at functions.
The inverse direction—i.e., well-typed terms always terminate—also holds because, intuitively, (finite, non-idempotent) intersection types ensure that each value is used only finitely many times, whereas non-terminating terms must refer to the same (recursive) value infinitely many times; see, for example,
(
5
)
for detail.

Now, suppose that we want to generalize the discussion we have just carried out to a programming language for effect handlers. Assume we start from a calculus in the style of
\HEPCF

(
13
)
. This calculus can be seen as an extension of the simply typed
\lambda
-calculus with two features. On the one hand, terms are allowed to perform algebraic operations, intended to model computational effects such as various forms of choice, global store read and write operations, exceptions, input and output. Each such operation
\sigma
can be invoked in the form
\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};y.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u})
, where the actual parameter
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
is supplied together with a
continuation

{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}
, which may depend on the output
x
produced by executing the operation. On the other hand, the language provides the ability for programs to
handle
these operations, that is, to specify precisely how they should be interpreted, in analogy with the classic mechanism of exception handling. Syntactically, handling is captured by a construct
\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
in which any operation executed by the computation
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
is managed according to the handler
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
, the latter being a collection of
operation clauses
, each corresponding to an algebraic operation.

For example, consider term
\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,\sigma(\mathsf{()};x.\mathsf{if}\;x\;\mathsf{then}\;\mathsf{return}(1)\;\mathsf{else}\;\mathsf{return}(2))
.
This term first performs the algebraic operation
\sigma
.
Suppose that, to handle it, the handler
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
provides an operation clause

\sigma(y;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
for
\sigma
.
Then, the handling transfers the control to the clause body
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
.
The variable
y
and
r
bound in
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
denote an argument and a continuation, respectively, given by an operation call to be handled—therefore, in the example term, they denote the argument
\mathsf{()}
and (the functional form) of the continuation
x.{\mathsf{if}\;x\;\mathsf{then}\;\mathsf{return}(1)\;\mathsf{else}\;\mathsf{return}(2)}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
only returns a value, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}=\mathsf{return}({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v})
for some value
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
, the whole term will also return
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
. In this case,
\sigma
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
work as exception raising and handling, respectively.
By contrast, if
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}=r\mkern 1.0mu\mathsf{true}
, which invokes the continuation
r
with Boolean value
\mathsf{true}
, then computation
\mathsf{if}\;\mathsf{true}\;\mathsf{then}\;\mathsf{return}(1)\;\mathsf{else}\;\mathsf{return}(2)
obtained by substituting the argument
\mathsf{true}
for the variable
x
in the continuation will be executed.
The handler
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
can also call the continuation multiple times using
different arguments (say,
\mathsf{true}
and
\mathsf{false}
).
This capability to resume continuations flexibly enables us to easily implement,
e.g., enumeration of all possible results of non-deterministic computations,
backtracking, mutable state, and transactions
(
43
)
.

In
\HEPCF
, computations receive types in the form
{\mathcal{E}},{\mathcal{F}}:=M_{E}
, where
M
is the type of the values returned by the computation and
E
is a signature type keeping track of the identity and behaviour of the algebraic operations the underlying term may produce. Handlers are viewed as
computation transformers
, and this view is justified by the typing rule for the construct
\mathsf{with}
-
\mathsf{handle}
, which installs an effect handler
{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}}
to handle the effect performed in a term
t
:

\mathtt{(hdl)}

\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}H}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}:\mathcal{F}\Rightarrow\mathcal{E}
\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\mathcal{F}
\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\mathcal{E}

The premise
\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}H}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}:\mathcal{F}\Rightarrow\mathcal{E}
tells us, in particular, that the handlers included in
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
are able to somehow
transform
any computation of type
\mathcal{F}
into a computation of type
\mathcal{E}
. This amounts to verifying that for each clause
\sigma(x;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
there exists a way of typing
t
in accordance with
\mathcal{F}
.
For example, the handler
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
in the aforementioned example can be assigned type
\prescript{}{\{\sigma:\mathsf{unit}\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}\mathsf{bool}\}}{\mathsf{int}}\Rightarrow\prescript{}{\emptyset}{\mathsf{int}}
, which means that
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
transforms computations that may perform algebraic operation
\sigma:\mathsf{unit}\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}\mathsf{bool}
, to computations that perform no operation.

Let us now suppose that we want to try to prove, for this type system, the subject expansion property. Among the reduction rules governing calculi such as
\HEPCF
, there is one that is particularly interesting, namely the following:

(2)

\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};y.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u})\mapsto_{\mathtt{hdl\mbox{-}ef}}{}{}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\{x\backslash{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\}\{r\backslash\lambda y.\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}\}

where the handler
{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}}
is assumed to provide a clause
\sigma(x;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
to interpret the algebraic operation
\sigma
.
This rule tells us that handling an algebraic operation
\sigma
by means of the effect handler
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
requires extracting from
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
the clause
\sigma(x;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
corresponding to
\sigma
and passing to its body
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
two objects, namely the parameter
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
and the
delimited continuation

{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}=\lambda y.\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}
.

Now, suppose that the RHS of reduction (
2
) is typable, say with a computation type
\mathcal{E}
, and that
we want to show that the same type can somehow be transferred to the LHS.
Collecting the types of the various occurrences of the value

{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
in the RHS into a single intersection type
\mathcal{M}
can
be done easily as sketched above, of course provided that intersections are present.
Dealing with the (possibly many) occurrences of the continuation
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}
is more
challenging, depending on whether the continuation occurs in the RHS.
First, assume that the continuation does
not
occur in the RHS.
In this case, we
cannot collect any type information about
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
from the RHS, except for the clause

\sigma(x;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
, which provides the term
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
in the RHS.
As a result, a typing derivation built for the LHS should only typecheck
\sigma
’s clause and
should
not
typecheck the other clauses.
To address this typing requirement on the LHS, as shown shortly, we introduce a
new form of computation types that keeps track of what algebraic operation a
term calls
first
, and the typing of a handler only typechecks the clauses
of the algebraic operations that will be called
necessarily
.
Next, suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
refers to the continuation.
In such a case, the only information we can collect from the occurrences of the
continuation
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}
in the RHS is:

\mathtt{(hdl)}

\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}H}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}:\mathcal{F}_{i}\Rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}
y:{\mathcal{N}_{i}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}:\mathcal{F}_{i}
y:{\mathcal{N}_{i}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}:\mathcal{E}_{i}

where
i
ranges over a finite set
I
indexing all occurrences
of the variable
r
in
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
. Here, the computation types
\mathcal{E}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i}
and the value type
\mathcal{N}_{i}
, which are the type information at some
occurrence of
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}
, can be
completely uncorrelated
with the type
information
\mathcal{E}_{j},\mathcal{F}_{j},\mathcal{N}_{j}
(
i\neq j
) at another occurrence of

{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}
.
We have to
turn them
into the typings of
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
and
\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};x.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u})
in the LHS.
We are therefore in a situation similar to the one described above concerning the anti-substitution lemma.

The solution to the aforementioned problem we propose in this paper consists in considering computation types that have a behavioral nature, that is, that aggregate the information contained in the types
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}_{i},\mathcal{F}_{i}
into a single composite type

(3)

\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{i}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I},

called a
computation type
. Computation types are behavioral, as they specify the algebraic operations terms give rise to first, here
\sigma
, together with the type of the parameters
to it, namely
\mathcal{M}
. The types
{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{i}}
, instead, are all the possible types we assign, in the spirit of intersection types, to the continuation. In the context of the LHS of reduction rule (
2
), the type (
3
) is the means by which we allow the handler
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
and the algebraic operations in
\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};x.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u})
to “talk to each other”. Defining computation types this way allows us to attribute a type to algebraic operations as follows:

\mathtt{(eff)}

\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}:\mathcal{M}
(y:\mathcal{N}_{i}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}:\mathcal{F}_{i})_{i\in I}
\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};y.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}):\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{i}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I}

It is therefore clear that the computation type we assign to the underlying term tells us which operation is being performed, what the type of its parameter is
but also
how the continuation
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}
will be used when
\sigma
is handled by
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
. But how is it possible to type the handler
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
itself? It would be ideal if it received a type of the form

\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{i}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{E}}
,
because this way the type would be preserved. And indeed, this turns out to be possible by means of the following typing rule:

\mathtt{(hdlr_{\sigma})}

x:\mathcal{M};r:\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\mathcal{E}
(\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}H}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}:\mathcal{F}_{i}\Rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i})_{i\in I}
\{\sigma(x;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\}\mathrel{\#}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}H}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\{\sigma(x;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}:\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{i}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I}\Rightarrow\mathcal{E}

namely the one we use in our intersection type system
\mathsf{HEBI}
.
This rule indicates that the clause for the algebraic operation performed first
has to be typechecked (the first premise) and the handler
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
has to be
typechecked against each type
\mathcal{F}_{i}\Rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}
(the second
premise) which matches with the use of the continuation in
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
.
The premise

\{\sigma(x;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\}\mathrel{\#}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
serves to ensure that, if a clause for
\sigma
is present in
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
, it has the same shape, namely
\sigma(x;r)\mapsto{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}}
. Observe that, in this situation, the
handlers
in the premise and in the conclusion of the rule may coincide. Nevertheless, these two occurrences can still be
typed
differently: the type in the premise collects the types corresponding to (possibly) other usages of the operations in
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
.
Therefore, if the continuation
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
calls algebraic operations (including

\sigma
), the clauses corresponding to them are typechecked in the second
premise.

Although our computation types require determining the first algebraic operations called,
intersection types allow typechecking terms with an undetermined order of operation calls.
For instance, consider a term

\lambda x.\mathsf{if}\;x\;\mathsf{then}\;\sigma_{1}(x_{1};{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}_{1}.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}_{1})\;\mathsf{else}\;\sigma_{2}(x_{2};{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}_{2}.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}_{2})
,
which calls
\sigma_{1}
or
\sigma_{2}
depending on a Boolean value
x
.
Given singleton types
\mathit{true}
and
\mathit{false}
that are only assigned to Boolean values
\mathtt{true}
and
\mathtt{false}
, respectively,
this term can be assigned an intersection type

\mathit{true}\rightarrow\prescript{\sigma_{1}[M_{1}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{N_{1i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{1i}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I}\;\cap\;\mathit{false}\rightarrow\prescript{\sigma_{2}[M_{2}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{N_{2j}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{2j}\}\!\!\}_{j\in J}~.

Finally, it is noteworthy that this typing mechanism allows assigning different
types to different calls to the same algebraic operation.
For example, consider a term

\sigma(1;x.\sigma(\mathtt{false};y.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}))
, to which
a computation type

\prescript{\sigma[\mathit{int}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{M_{i}\rightarrow\prescript{\sigma[\mathit{bool}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{N_{ij}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{ij}\}\!\!\}_{j\in J}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I}

may be assigned. This computation type indicates that the first and second calls to the
operation
\sigma
may be typed differently.
This typing is impossible in the calculi such as
\HEPCF
and others
(
41
;
42
;
4
;
24
;
34
;
59
;
56
)
,
where all the calls to the same algebraic operation handled by the same handler have to be
typed in the same way.
Our computation types can distinguish different calls to the same operation
since they are aware of the
order
of the calls, namely, they capture
(finite)
computation trees
of algebraic operations produced by terms.

But why can such an intersection type system be useful in the context of HOMC, in particular when we are interested in decidability problems? The idea is quite simple. Suppose we want to check whether a simply typed
\lambda
-term
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}={\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}
of Boolean type evaluates to one of the two Boolean values, for example to
\mathit{true}
. One can proceed by systematically checking whether, given a type
A
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
has type
A\rightarrow\mathit{bool}
and
w
has type
A
, there exists a
refinement

\mathcal{A}
of
A
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
has type
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathit{true}
and
w
has type
\mathcal{A}
. This notion of refinement should, on the one hand, provide precise information about the value to which terms evaluate and, on the other hand, support a form of exhaustive search. Classical idempotent intersection types
(
11
)
enjoy both of these properties. On the other hand, the behavioural types introduced in this work, although not satisfying these properties with respect to
\HEPCF
, naturally suggest a new type system, called
\mathsf{HEB}
, for which the HOMC problem is shown to be decidable, and which will be introduced in Section
5
below.

3.
Effect Handlers, (Un)typed

In this section, we introduce the preliminaries related to the calculi
\mathsf{HE}
and
\HEPCF
, the latter being a typed version of the former. These calculi provide, in addition to the usual constructions of a
\lambda
-calculus in fine-grain call-by-value form, recursion, integers, and, of course, algebraic effects and handlers.

Terms

Given a countably infinite set
\mathcal{X}
of variables (
x,y,z,\ldots
) and a countably infinite set
\mathcal{O}
of operations (
\sigma,\sigma_{i}\ldots
),
the set
\Lambda
of
terms

(
p\ldots
) is given by the following inductive definitions:

\begin{array}[]{crcl}\textbf{(Computations)}&{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t},{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u},{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}&\coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}\;|\;\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};x.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t})\;|\;\mathsf{return}({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v})\;|\;\mathsf{let}\,x\doteq{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\,\mathsf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}\\
&&&\;|\;\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\;|\;\mathsf{case}({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}_{n})\\[5.69046pt]
\textbf{(Values)}&{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v},{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}&\coloneqq&\underline{n}\;|\;x\;|\;\lambda x.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\;|\;\mathsf{fix}\,x.{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\\[5.69046pt]
\textbf{(Handlers)}&{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}&\coloneqq&\{\mathsf{return}(x)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\}\cup\{\sigma_{\mkern-2.0mui}(x;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}_{i}\;|\;0<i\leq n\}\par\\[5.69046pt]
\textbf{(Terms)}&p&\coloneqq&{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\end{array}

The set
\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
of
computations

(
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t},{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}\ldots
) includes

applications

{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}
composed of
a
function

{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
applied to an
argument

{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}
,

effect operations

\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};x.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t})
composed of
an
algebraic operation

\sigma
applied to a

parameter

{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
and followed by a
continuation

{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}

in which the variable
x
is to be substituted with the result of the operation,

return operations

\mathsf{return}({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v})
composed of
a
resulting value

{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}

(note that a return operation can be seen as a particular
algebraic operation with no continuation, furthermore, note that the purpose of the return construct is to embed values in computations),

let bindings

\mathsf{let}\,x\doteq{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\,\mathsf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}
composed of
a
definition

{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
for a value
x
followed
by a
continuation

{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}
,

handling

\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
composed of
a
handler

{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
interpreting a
handled computation

{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
,
and
case analysis

\mathsf{case}({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}_{n})
composed of
a
matched value

{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
and
possible continutations

({\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}_{k})_{k\leq n}
. This construct represents a branching computation depending on a (bounded) integer.
The set
\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\texttt{Val}}}
of
values

(
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v},{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}\ldots
) includes

positive integers

\underline{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,

variables

x\in\mathcal{X}
,

abstractions

\lambda x.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
composed of
a
body

{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
and an
abstracted variable

x
,
and
fixpoint

\mathsf{fix}\,x.{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
composed of
a
body

{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
and a
recursive variable

x
.
Finally, each
handler

{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}\texttt{Hdl}}}
contains

effect clauses

\{\sigma(x;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\}

for different operations
\sigma
with an
interpretation

{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}

based on the
parameter

x
of the operation and the

continuation

r
. Each handler
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}

necessarly contains an interpretation
\mathsf{return}(x)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}

of the return operation which has no continuation.

An effect operation
\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};y.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t})
can be
interpreted as
performing
the operation
\sigma

with the parameter
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
as input, then using the output
y
of the
operation in the following computation
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
. Correspondingly,
an effect clause
\sigma(x;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}
of a handler
defines an
interpretation
of the operation
\sigma
. The variable
x
corresponds
to the parameter
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
of the effect operation, and the variable
r

is a function corresponding to the computation
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
performs after
\sigma

depending on the output
y
of
\sigma
.
Binding is defined as usual. In particular, notice that
in each clause
\sigma(x;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
of a handler
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
,
the variables
x
and
r
are bound in the interpretation
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
.

Operational Semantics.

To define the operational semantics of

\mathsf{HE}
terms, we first need to introduce
evaluation contexts
, which are terms containing exactly one occurrence of the
hole

\diamond
that should be filled by terms to be reduced. Formally, they are
inductively defined as follows.

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}\textbf{(Evaluation Ctxt)}&\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}&\coloneqq&\diamond\;|\;\mathsf{let}\,x\doteq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}\,\mathsf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\;|\;\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}\\
\end{array}

Moreover, we write
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}\!\left<t\right>
for the term obtained by replacing
the only occurrence of
\diamond
in
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}
with the term
t
.

We denote by
p\{x\backslash{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\}
the term obtained by
the usual (capture-avoiding) meta-level
substitution

of the value
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
for all free occurrences of the variable
x
in
the term
p
. The usual notions of free variables,
\alpha
-conversion and substitution are extended to the set of terms
\Lambda
, and terms are
identified up to
\alpha
-conversion.

\begin{array}[]{clll}(\lambda x.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t})\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}&\mapsto_{\mathtt{\beta}}&{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\{x\backslash{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\}\\[2.84544pt]
(\mathsf{fix}\,x.{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v})\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}&\mapsto_{\mathtt{fix}}&({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\{x\backslash\mathsf{fix}\,x.{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\})\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}\\[2.84544pt]
\mathsf{case}(\underline{n};{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}_{m})&\mapsto_{\mathtt{case}}&{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}_{n}&\text{when }0<n\leq m\\[2.84544pt]
\mathsf{let}\,x\doteq\mathsf{return}({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v})\,\mathsf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}&\mapsto_{\mathtt{let\mbox{-}ret}}&{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\{x\backslash{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\}\\[2.84544pt]
\mathsf{let}\,x\doteq\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};y.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t})\,\mathsf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}&\mapsto_{\mathtt{let\mbox{-}ef}}&\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};y.\mathsf{let}\,x\doteq{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\,\mathsf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u})\\[2.84544pt]
\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,\mathsf{return}({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v})&\mapsto_{\mathtt{hdl\mbox{-}ret}}&{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\{x\backslash{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\}&\text{when }\mathsf{return}(x)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\in{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\\[2.84544pt]
\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};y.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u})&\mapsto_{\mathtt{hdl\mbox{-}ef}}&{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\{x\backslash{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\}\{r\backslash\lambda y.\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}\}&\text{when }\sigma(x;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\in{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\end{array}

Figure 1.
Reduction rules for
\mathsf{HE}

With this,
the seven rewriting rules defined in
Figure

1

establish reduction for
\mathsf{HE}
, in which we implicitly assume the terms to be closed. We write
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\mapsto{}{}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}
if
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
rewrites to
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}
.
The first four rules (
\mathtt{\beta},\mathtt{fix},\mathtt{case},\mathtt{let\mbox{-}ret}
)
correspond to the usual reduction rules for a
\lambda
-calculus with
recursive functions.
The
\mathtt{let\mbox{-}ef}
-rule states that any let binding with an effect
as definition can be simplified by permuting the let binding with the
effect operation, therefore highlighting that the operation
\sigma
is meant
to be performed first.
The
\mathtt{hdl\mbox{-}ef}
-rule states that any handling with an effect operation
as handled computation can be simplified into the interpretation
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}

for the operation clause of the handler
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
, where the occurrences of
the parameter
x
are replaced with the argument value . Similarly
for the
\mathtt{hdl\mbox{-}ret}
rule, which states that, when a handled computation returns a value, the return clause provided by the effect handler is evaluated with the returned value.
Moreover, for the
\mathtt{hdl\mbox{-}ef}
-rule, the occurrences of the continuation
r
are also replaced with the
handling of
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}
by
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
, abstracted over the output
variable
y
of the effect.

The
\mathtt{hdl\mbox{-}ret}
- and
\mathtt{hdl\mbox{-}ef}
-rules illustrate how interpretations
define the performance of return and effect operations. On the other hand,
with the
\mathtt{let\mbox{-}ef}
-rule, the first performed effect bubbles up to the top of
the computation. This rule works in tandem with the
\mathtt{hdl\mbox{-}ef}
-rule
to associate deeply embedded effect operations to their interpretations
using only local rules. Using the evaluation contexts, we can now define
reduction as followed.

Definition 3.0 (Reduction).

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t},{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
be closed terms.
We say that
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}

reduces to

{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}
, written

{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\rightarrow{}{}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}
iff there is an evaluation context

\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}\!\left<{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}\right>
and

{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}\!\left<{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}r}\right>
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}\mapsto{}{}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}r}
.
We write
\twoheadrightarrow{}{}
for the reflexive and transitive closure of the reduction
relation
\rightarrow{}{}
.

Note that given the way evaluation contexts are defined, reduction is deterministic and call-by-value.

Example 3.1.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}=\{\mathsf{return}(x)\mapsto\sigma(x;y.\mathsf{return}(x))\}
. Then,

\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,\mathsf{return}(\underline{5})\mapsto_{\mathtt{hdl\mbox{-}ret}}{}{}\sigma(\underline{5};y.\mathsf{return}(\underline{5}))~.

And for
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}=\mathsf{let}\,z\doteq\diamond\,\mathsf{in}\,\mathsf{return}(z)
,

\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}\!\left<\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,\mathsf{return}(\underline{5})\right>\rightarrow{}{}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}\!\left<\sigma(\underline{5};y.\mathsf{return}(\underline{5}))\right>~.

The just defined calculus, called
\mathsf{HE}
, is genuinely untyped, and as such does not satisfy any of those desirable properties like termination or progress. In fact, type clashes can easily occur. The calculus
\mathsf{HE}
, however, is Turing-powerful, and its expressive power from the standpoint of computability theory would remain unchanged in the absence of effects and handlers, as well as without recursion. Consequently, most verification problems, including reachability problems, remain undecidable in this calculus. It is anyway worth clarifying what we mean by reachability here; we in fact adopt a very simple definition, which does not capture reachability in its full generality, but which is fine for our purposes. Given a closed term
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
, the
reachability problem
asks whether there exists a value
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\twoheadrightarrow{}{}\mathsf{return}({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v})
, that is, whether the evaluation of
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
reaches a value. We note that, e.g., the halting problem can be easily encoded as one such reachability problem.

Simple Types

In this section, we restrict our attention to a
subset of terms, with the aim of guaranteeing a form
of type safety closely following a simply-typed discipline.
We associate with each computation an effect context specifying
the input and output types of each algebraic operation.
The grammar of simple types then becomes the following.

\begin{array}[]{crcl}\textbf{(Value Types)}&\mathsf{L},\mathsf{M},\mathsf{N}&\coloneqq&\mathsf{n}\;|\;\mathsf{M}\rightarrow\mathsf{U}\\
\textbf{(Computation Types)}&\mathsf{U},\mathsf{S}&\coloneqq&\prescript{}{\mathtt{E}}{\mathsf{M}}\\
\textbf{(Handler Types)}&\mathsf{H}&\coloneqq&\mathsf{S}\Rightarrow\mathsf{U}\\
\textbf{(Effect Contexts)}&\mathtt{E},\mathtt{F}&\coloneqq&\emptyset\;|\;\{\sigma:\mathsf{M}\mathrel{\rightsquigarrow}\mathsf{N}\}\cup\mathtt{E}\\
\textbf{(General Types)}&\mathsf{T}&\coloneqq&\mathsf{M}\;|\;\mathsf{U}\;|\;\mathsf{H}\\
\end{array}

The integer type
2
2

2

We could have considered a single, infinite type of all positive integers, but we prefer to work with finite base types here and throughout the remainder of this article, in view of the discussions about the higher-order model checking problem.

\mathsf{n}
is inhabited by all natural numbers
in the range
[1,n]
. The
computation type
\prescript{}{\mathtt{E}}{\mathsf{M}}
indicates both the type
\mathsf{M}
of the
returned value, and the specification
\mathtt{E}
of the effects possibly used, given as an
effect context
, namely a signature specifying for every operation
\sigma
both the type
\mathsf{M}
of the argument and the type
\mathsf{N}
of the result, to be passed to the continuation.

We define three distinct typing judgments for values, computations, and handlers, respectively. The notations for these simple type judgments are as follows, and the corresponding inference rules are given
in
Figure

2
.

\end{array}

Figure 2.
\HEPCF
typing rules.

Most of the typing rules of
\HEPCF
are standard, as they closely follow those of the simply typed
\lambda
-calculus. Some of them, however, deserve brief discussion. Terms of the form
\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};x.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t})
are assigned a type by rule (
\mathtt{eff}

). Observe that, while the type of the parameter
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
and that of the variable
x
must be consistent with the effect context
E
, the return type of the computation
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
is completely arbitrary, and becomes the type of the whole term.
Furthermore, when typing handlers, each algebraic operation is analyzed separately, and it is therefore possible to assign exactly one type to each of them. This is fully consistent with the monomorphic nature of the type system.

\HEPCF

as we have just introduced it is type safe, which can be easily shown by proving Subject Reduction and Progress. Its expressiveness is limited, in particular due to the fact that the base types are all finite. One might think that this restriction, which in
\mathsf{PCF}
leads to the
decidability
of the HOMC problem for a very broad class of properties, namely those captured by MSO formulas, would also make this problem decidable for
\HEPCF
. Unfortunately, Dal Lago and Ghyselen showed
(
13
)
that this is not the case, and that the HOMC is undecidable in
\HEPCF
already for mere reachability properties. (The latter, by the way, can be formulated for
\HEPCF
in a completely analogous way to what we did for
\mathsf{HE}
earlier in this section.) This is proved by simulating the standard inductive type of integers through algebraic effects and handlers. We are interested in understanding the deep reason behind the jump in expressiveness between finitary
\mathsf{PCF}
and
\HEPCF
. The kind of behavioral intersection typing we introduce in the next section will indeed provide a new point of view on this issue, which we defer to Section
6
.

4.
Behavioral Intersection Typing

It is time to introduce our new type discipline of behavioral intersection typing, which will result in the
\mathsf{HEBI}
type system. After formalizing it, we show its metatheoretical properties.

4.1.
The
\mathsf{HEBI}
Type System

We first define the type language of the
\mathsf{HEBI}
type system.
As with intersection types, values are typed with
a set
of types.
This corresponds to the term being typable with
every type
in the set.
Moreover, the type system is
behavioral
in nature, keeping track of
the effect produced and of their order.
The grammars of
\mathsf{HEBI}
typing is defined as follows.

\begin{array}[]{crcl}\textbf{(Value Types)}&\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}&\coloneqq&\left\{\,\right\}\;|\;\left\{\mathsf{n}\right\}\;|\;\left\{\mathcal{M}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}\\
\textbf{(Computation Types)}&\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}&\coloneqq&\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}\;|\;\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I}\par\\
\textbf{(Handler Types)}&\mathcal{H}&\coloneqq&\mathcal{E}\Rightarrow\mathcal{F}\par\\
\textbf{(General Types)}&\tau,\rho&\coloneqq&\mathcal{M}\;|\;\mathcal{E}\;|\;\mathcal{H}\par\end{array}

where
I
is a finite set.
A
value type

\mathcal{M}
caracterizes how a value
can be used by a computation. It can either be
an
interger type

\{\mathsf{n}\}
associated a specific value
\underline{n}
,
a
function type

\left\{\mathcal{M}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}

associated to functions that compute for every
i\in I
a process of type
\mathcal{E}_{i}

when applied to a value of type
\mathcal{M}_{i}
,
or the
empty type

\left\{\,\right\}
corresponding to any unused value
3
3

3

We tacitly assume that
\left\{\,\right\}
and
\left\{\mathcal{M}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}\right\}_{i\in\emptyset}
denote the same type.
.
A
computation type

\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I}

describes the tree of branching effects performed by
a computation. Its
root
(or starting) effect
is the operation
\sigma
with a parameter of type
\mathcal{M}
.
The
children
types
\mathcal{E}_{i}
of this root
are indexed by the possible types
\mathcal{N}_{i}
of the operation’s output
(each of these children corresponding to a possible process of the
effect’s continuation). Similarly, the computation
type
\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}
corresponds to a computation returning
a value of type
\mathcal{M}
. A
handler type

\mathcal{E}\Rightarrow\mathcal{F}
describes handlers
transforming computations of type
\mathcal{E}
, into
ones of type
\mathcal{F}
, as in
\HEPCF
. We note, however, that whereas in

\mathsf{HEBI}
a computation type is behavioral and a handler type therefore configures
itself as a proper tree transformer, in
\HEPCF
a computation type only captures
the type of the parameter to the return command, if any, the computation
will call, together with a typing for the algebraic operations.
We give in
Figure

3
the visualisation of a computational
\mathsf{HEBI}
type that can be associated to the computation
\sigma(\underline{3};x.\mathsf{case}(x;\mathsf{return}(x),\ \delta(\underline{2};f.f\mkern 1.0mu\underline{3})))
. A type
\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I}
is represented as a tree whose root is
\sigma[\mathcal{M}]
, whose children are
\mathcal{E}_{i}
, and whose edges are labeled by the corresponding
\mathcal{N}_{i}
.

\sigma[{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\mathsf{3}}]
\mathsf{return}[{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\mathsf{1}}]
\mathsf{1}
\delta[{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\left\{\,\right\}}]
\mathsf{return}[{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\mathsf{5}}]
\left\{\left\{\mathsf{3}\right\}\rightarrow\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathsf{5}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}\right\}
\delta[{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\mathsf{3}}]
\left\{\left\{\mathsf{3}\right\}\rightarrow\prescript{\delta[\mathsf{3}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\}\!\!\}\right\}
\mathsf{2}

Figure 3.
Visualisation of the
\mathsf{HEBI}
type

\prescript{\sigma[\mathsf{3}]\!}{}{\left\{\!\!\left\{\left\{\mathsf{1}\right\}\rightarrow\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathsf{1}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}\ \middle|\ \left\{\mathsf{2}\right\}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}\right\}\!\!\right\}}
,
where
\mathcal{E}=\prescript{\delta[\left\{\,\right\}]\!}{}{\left\{\!\!\left\{\left\{\left\{\mathsf{3}\right\}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\right\}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\ \middle|\ \left\{\left\{\mathsf{3}\right\}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}\right\}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}\right\}\!\!\right\}}
,

\mathcal{F}=\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathsf{5}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}
and
\mathcal{G}=\prescript{\delta[\mathsf{3}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\}\!\!\}
.

As we said, values in
\mathsf{HEBI}
are typed as sets modelling intersections. Therefore, given two value types
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}
, we can define their union
\mathcal{M}\cup\mathcal{N}
. It is important to notice, however, that the value
\underline{n}
can receive the value types
\left\{\right\}
and
\left\{n\right\}
. Thus, the union
\left\{m\right\}\cup\left\{n\right\}
is defined if and only if
n=m
. The union operator
\cup
is therefore partial.
We also define a subset relation on value types in the standard manner, written
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
.

A type context
\Gamma=x_{1}:\mathcal{M}_{1};\dots x_{n}:\mathcal{M}_{n}

is a total function from the set
\mathcal{X}
of variables to the set of
value types, where every variable
x_{i}
is associated to
\mathcal{M}_{i}
, and
any
y
not mentioned explicitly in
\Gamma
, is associated to the
empty type
\left\{\,\right\}
. We define the sum of two type contexts

\Gamma+\Gamma^{\prime}
as

\{x:\mathcal{M}\cup\mathcal{N}\;|\;\text{where $x:\mathcal{M}\in\Gamma$ and $x:\mathcal{N}\in\Gamma^{\prime}$}\}
. Notice
that, once again this is a
partial
binary operation on contexts,
since
\cup
is partial on value types.

Definition 4.0.

Let
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}
be type contexts. We say
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma
iff for every
x:\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
, one has
x:\mathcal{M}\in\Gamma
with
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
. We write
Sub(\Gamma)
for the set of type contexts
\Gamma^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma
.

Typing jugements

As in
\HEPCF
, there are three kinds of typing jugements, written as follows.

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\Gamma\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\mathcal{E}&&\Gamma\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}:\mathcal{M}&&\Gamma\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}H}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}:\mathcal{H}\end{array}

A typing jugement of the form
\Gamma\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\mathcal{E}

implies that, in the context
\Gamma
, evaluating the computation

{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
produces a computation tree coherent with the one described
by
\mathcal{E}
.
A typing jugement
\Gamma\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}:\mathcal{M}

indicates that the value
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
can be used as prescribed by
every

type in the set
\mathcal{M}
(in the context
\Gamma
).
Finally, the jugement
\Gamma\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}H}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}:\mathcal{E}\Rightarrow\mathcal{F}

indicates that the handler
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
can transform a computation
of type
\mathcal{E}
into one of type
\mathcal{F}
in the context
\Gamma
.
The typing rules are defined in
Figure

4
.
Again, we write
\Gamma\vdash p:\tau
for one of the three judgments,
depending on the nature of
p
and
\tau
.
We also write
\Pi\mathrel{\triangleright}\Gamma\vdash p:\tau
if the jugement

\Gamma\vdash p:\tau
is the conclusion of the proof tree
\Pi
.
The typing rules use also the two auxilary relations, to which
we will devote the next two paragraphs. One of these relations is defined in
Figure

5
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\\[-5.69046pt]

4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\mathsf{case}({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}_{1},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}_{n}):\mathcal{E}$}}\par}\par}}}$}&&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip }$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Gamma\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern \end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 50.2736pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower
