\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 52.52242pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\text{$\mathtt{(hdlr_{ret})}$ }$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Gamma;y:\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\mathcal{E}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\{\mathsf{return}(y)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\}\mathrel{\#}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}$}}}}\hbox{$\Gamma\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}H}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\{\mathsf{return}(y)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}:\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}\Rightarrow\mathcal{E}$}}\par}\par}}}$}\\[14.22636pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 72.25232pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\text{$\mathtt{(hdlr_{\sigma})}$ }$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Gamma;y:\mathcal{M};r:\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\mathcal{E}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$(\Gamma_{i}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}H}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}:\mathcal{F}_{i}\Rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{i})_{i\in I}$}}\hbox{$\{\sigma(y;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\}\mathrel{\#}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}$}}}}}\hbox{$\Gamma+(+_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i})\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}H}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\{\sigma(y;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}:\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{i}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I}\Rightarrow\mathcal{E}$}}\par}\par}}}$}\end{array}\\[48.36958pt]
\end{array}

Figure 4.
\mathsf{HEBI}
, typing rules.

\begin{array}[]{c}\\[-8.5359pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 67.66145pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{$\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}\mathrel{-\>\!\!\!\!\left\{\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}\right\}\>\!\!\!\!\rightarrow}\mathcal{E}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\par}\par}}}$}\qquad\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 71.13652pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\left(\mathcal{F}_{i}\mathrel{-\>\!\!\!\!\left\{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{k}^{i}\right\}\>\!\!\!\!\overrightarrow{\,\vskip-8.69995pt{}^{k\in K_{i}}\;}}\mathcal{E}_{i}\right)_{i\in I}$}}\hbox{$\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{i}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I}\mathrel{-\>\!\!\!\!\left\{\mathcal{M}_{k}^{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{k}^{i}\right\}\>\!\!\!\!\overrightarrow{\,\vskip-8.69995pt{}^{k\in K_{i},i\in I}\;}}\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I}$}}\par}\par}}}$}\\[19.91684pt]
\end{array}

Figure 5.
\mathsf{HEBI}
, Leaf Replacement.

Handler Compatibility

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}\texttt{Hdl}}}
be an handler. The relation
\{\sigma(y;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\}\mathrel{\#}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
holds if for any
\{\sigma(y;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}\}\in{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}={\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}
. This relation certifies that the clause
\sigma(y;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}

is not in conflict with any clause for
\sigma
in
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
. The handler compatibility on
\mathsf{return}
clauses is defined similarly:
\{\mathsf{return}(y)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\}\mathrel{\#}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
holds if for any
\{\mathsf{return}(y)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}\}\in{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}={\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}
. In this case, the relation certifies that
\mathsf{return}(y)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
is the only
\mathsf{return}
clause in
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
. Observe that the handler compatibility relation is not
required in
\HEPCF
, where the handling of all operations is done through terms that
are forced to adhere to the same (
non
-behavioral) return type and to the same signature.
As a consequence, there would be no meaningful reason to type them multiple times. This situation
changes in
\mathsf{HEBI}
, where behavioral information becomes an integral part of the type and handling
terms can be thus treated polymorphically.

Leaf Replacement

\mathcal{E}\mathrel{-\>\!\!\!\!\left\{\mathcal{M}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{i}\right\}\>\!\!\!\!\overrightarrow{\,\vskip-8.69995pt{}^{i\in I}\;}}\mathcal{F}
, is a relation stating that the type
\mathcal{E}
is transformed
to the type
\mathcal{F}
by replacing each type
\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}_{i}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}
occurring
as a leaf of
\mathcal{E}
with the corresponding type
\mathcal{G}_{i}
. Intuitively, this represents
the composition of computation trees produced by sequential terms.
To see it, consider a term
{\mathsf{let}\,x\doteq{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\,\mathsf{in}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}}
with the term
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
having
a type
\mathcal{E}
.
When
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
terminates, its continuation
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}
refers to a value
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
returned by
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
.
The type of
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
matches
\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}_{i}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}
for some
i
in
\mathcal{E}

because
\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}_{i}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}
corresponds to a
leaf
of the
computation tree produced by
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
, namely, a branch of its evaluation
ending at the leaf.
Suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}
also produces another tree ot type
\mathcal{G}_{i}
using
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
.
Then, the produced tree represents the continuation of the process that returns the value
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
.
Therefore, replacing the leaf corresponding to the return process by
\mathcal{G}_{i}

results in composing the tree of
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
with that of
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}
.
The leaf replacement relation implements this idea formally.

Figure

6
illustrates how it works on a simple example.
One may note that it is possible to have identical leaves that are each replaced
by different trees (ie.
\mathcal{M}_{i}=\mathcal{M}_{j}
with
\mathcal{G}_{i}\neq\mathcal{G}_{j}
).

\sigma[\mathcal{M}]
\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]
\mathcal{N}_{1}
\sigma[\mathcal{N}]
\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}_{2}]
\mathcal{N}_{2}
\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]
\mathcal{N}_{3}
\mathcal{N}_{2}
\mathcal{M}_{1}\rightarrow\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}_{1}]\!}{}{\left\{\!\!\left\{\begin{matrix}\mathcal{N}_{1}\rightarrow\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}\\
\mathcal{N}_{2}\rightarrow\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}\end{matrix}\right\}\!\!\right\}}
\mathcal{M}_{1}\rightarrow\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\left\{\!\!\left\{\mathcal{N}_{2}\rightarrow\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}\right\}\!\!\right\}}
\mathcal{M}_{2}\rightarrow\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{N}_{2}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}
\sigma[\mathcal{M}]
\sigma[\mathcal{M}]
\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]
\mathcal{N}_{2}
\mathcal{N}_{1}
\sigma[\mathcal{N}]
\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{N}_{2}]
\mathcal{N}_{2}
\sigma[\mathcal{M}_{1}]
\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}_{1}]
\mathcal{N}_{1}
\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}]
\mathcal{N}_{2}
\mathcal{N}_{3}
\mathcal{N}_{2}

Figure 6.
\mathsf{HEBI}
Leaf Replacement: an Example

Most of the typing rules presented in
Figure

5

just capture our intention explained thus far, but a few remarks might help
understanding some of the most unusual rules.
First, notice that the rules
\mathtt{(var)}

and
\mathtt{(int)}

do not explicitly admit weakening. The context is intended to record the types associated with the different occurrences of variables. If a variable is weakened, this indicates that it is not used, which is represented by assigning it the empty type
\{\}
. Indeed, as already noted, the environment
\Gamma
is a total mapping and any variable which is not explicitly mentioned in context expressions is assumed to be mapped to the empty type. Consequently, when a variable does not occur free in a term, the environment maps it to the empty type. In premise of the rule
\mathtt{(fixb)}

, we explicitly write
x:\{\}
to emphasize that
x
is required to be mapped precisely to
\{\}
in the premise.
Moreover, if a typing rule derivation consists of multiple typing subderivations,
the type contexts of the subderivations are
summed
in the conclusion to collect
all the uses of variables in the subderivations.
Fixpoints are typed by two rules:
\mathtt{(fixb)}

and

\mathtt{(fixr)}

.
The former corresponds to the “base case,” hence assuming recursive variables to be unused by assigning the empty type to it.
The latter corresponds to the “inductive case,” recursively checking a fixpiont
\mathsf{fix}\,x.{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
against a type
\mathcal{N}
if
the body
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
is typechecked under the assignment of
\mathcal{N}
to the recursive variable
x
.
Since typing rules are inductively interpreted, the typechecking process for fixpoints can
apply
\mathtt{(fixr)}

only finitely many times, so, in turn, recursive
variables can be used only finitely many times.
This restriction leads to the guarantee of termination for any well-typed terms.
Finally, the typing rules for handlers (
\mathtt{(hdlr_{ret})}

and

\mathtt{(hdlr_{\sigma})}

) only typecheck the clause corresponding to the
operation (including
\mathsf{return}(
)) performed first in the underlying term being handled, as explained
in
Section

2
; if the continuation relies on other clauses, they
are typechecked
within
the typing derivations for the continuation.
Finally, by examining rule
\mathtt{(hdlr_{\sigma})}

, we also see why we defined
\{\sigma(y;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\}\mathrel{\#}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
in this way, rather than simply requiring that
{\sigma(y;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}}
does not occur in
{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}
. As noted earlier, the handlers appearing in the premises and in the conclusion of the rule may coincide. This makes it possible to type multiple uses of the same effect clause.

4.2.
Metatheoretical Properties

Now that the
\mathsf{HEBI}
type system has been properly defined,
we study its properties. Notably, as with intersection types
(
7
)
,
it should verify both subject
reduction
and subject
expansion
.
In other words, the
\mathsf{HEBI}
typability of a computation
should be conserved through reduction and expansion
(the opposite of reduction).
Subject reduction ensures that the
\mathsf{HEBI}
type
system is type sound, while subject expansion would ensure that it is expressive enough. Furthermore, to ensure safety, the system should verify a form of progess (
Lemma

4.7
), so that closed computation terms are either in the form of a return statement, a
\sigma
-operation, or can be reduced,
i.e.
there are no spurious closed and irreducible computations.
In order to prove these properties, let us first note a few
useful lemmas about
\mathsf{HEBI}
types.

4.3.
Basic Properties

Lemmas

4.1
and
4.2

given below ensure that value types indeed act as intersections
of their elements.
In other words, terms typed with a value type
\left\{\rho_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}

are exactly terms typed with
\left\{\rho_{j}\right\}_{j\in J}
for
every
J\subseteq I
. Furthermore, the empty type corresponds to an
empty intersection, and therefore should include all terms.

Lemma 4.1 (
\mathsf{HEBI}
, Splitting Lemma).

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\texttt{Val}}}
such that

\Xi\mathrel{\triangleright}\Gamma\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}:\mathcal{M}
and
I
such that
\mathcal{M}=\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}\mathcal{M}_{i}
.
Then for each
i\in I
there exist a derivation

\Pi_{i}\mathrel{\triangleright}\Gamma_{i}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}:\mathcal{M}_{i}
such that
\Gamma=+_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i}
.

Lemma 4.2 (
\mathsf{HEBI}
, Intersection Lemma).

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\texttt{Val}}}
be a value and
I
a set,
such that for every
i\in I
, there is a
\Pi_{i}
such that

\Pi_{i}\mathrel{\triangleright}\Gamma_{i}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}:\mathcal{M}_{i}
.
If
+_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i}
and
\bigcup\limits_{i\in I}\mathcal{M}_{i}
are defined,
then there exists
\Pi\mathrel{\triangleright}+_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}:\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathcal{M}_{i}
.

Both of these lemmas can be proved by induction on the structure of the derivations
that are assumed to exist by hypothesis.

In turn, these results constitute an essential intermediate step in the proof of a pair of key lemmas concerning the relationship between typability and the substitution operation. The first of these results is the classical Substitution Lemma, which states that the operation preserves typing in the forward direction.

Lemma 4.3 (
\mathsf{HEBI}
, Substitution Lemma).

Let
p\in\Lambda
be any term and
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\texttt{Val}}}
closed such that

\Pi\mathrel{\triangleright}\Gamma;x:\mathcal{M}\vdash p:\tau
and

\Pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}}\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}:\mathcal{M}
.
Then there exists

\Xi\mathrel{\triangleright}\Gamma_{p}\vdash p\{x\backslash{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\}:\tau
.

Proof Sketch.

As usual, the proof of the substitution lemma proceeds by induction on the structure of the term into which substitution is performed; in this case, one can equivalently proceed by induction on the structure of
\Pi
. The argument is mostly standard: it is perhaps only worth noting that, in those inductive cases concerning rules with more than one premise, Lemma
4.1
is used in an essential way.
∎

Contrary to what happens in most traditional type systems, a result dual to Lemma
4.3
also holds here, stating that typability is preserved in the backward direction as well.

Lemma 4.4 (
\mathsf{HEBI}
, Anti-substitution Lemma).

Let
p\in\Lambda

be a term and
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\texttt{Val}}}
a closed value.
If
\Pi\mathrel{\triangleright}\Gamma\vdash p\{x\backslash{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\}:\tau
, then there exists a type
\mathcal{M}
such that

\Pi_{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}:\mathcal{M}
and

\Pi_{p}\mathrel{\triangleright}\Gamma,x:\mathcal{M}\vdash p:\tau
.

Proof Sketch.

In this case, it is convenient to build the proof as an induction on the structure of
p
. The base case is therefore the one in which
p
is a variable, and in that case the thesis follows trivially from the hypothesis concerning
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
. The most interesting inductive cases, once again, are those concerning typing rules with at least two premises. In such cases, Lemma
4.2
is crucial, and its hypotheses are satisfied precisely because the value
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
is closed and therefore must necessarily be either an abstraction or a ground value. This ensures that the types we assign to
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
are mutually compatible.
∎

The last auxiliary lemma we consider is about evaluation contexts, and can be proved itself by induction on the structure of derivations. It tells
us that from any type derivation for a closed term in the form
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}\!\left<{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\right>
one can extract one for
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
.

Lemma 4.5 (
\mathsf{HEBI}
, Context Typing).

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
and

\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}
a computation context such that

\Pi\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}\!\left<{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\right>:\mathcal{E}
.
Then there exists an
\mathsf{HEBI}
-type
\mathcal{F}

such that
\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\mathcal{F}
and
for every
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}
such that
\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}:\mathcal{F}
, then it exists

\Xi\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}E}}\!\left<{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}\right>:\mathcal{E}
.

Using the previous lemmas, we may now show that
\mathsf{HEBI}
typing
is preserved along reduction and expansion. These properties
are particularly important because they certify that
\mathsf{HEBI}
types
express properties that are stable by reduction,
but also that these properties are precise enough to be passed on to
any expansion. Thanks to
Lemma

4.5
,
it suffices to consider the seven rewrite rules presented in
Figure

1
.

Lemma 4.6 (
\mathsf{HEBI}
, Subject Reduction and Expansion).

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t},{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
closed terms such that
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\rightarrow{}{}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}
. There
exists

\Pi\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\mathcal{E}

if and only if there exists

\Pi^{\prime}\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}:\mathcal{E}
.

We can also prove, by induction on the derivation, the following Progress Lemma.

Lemma 4.7 (
\mathsf{HEBI}
, Progress).

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
be a closed term such that
\Pi\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\mathcal{E}
. Then either there exists
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\rightarrow{}{}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
is a normal form computation, that is, either
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}=\mathsf{return}({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v})
or
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}=\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};x.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u})
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\texttt{Val}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
.

4.4.
Reducibility, Reachability, and Termination

Thanks to Lemma
4.6
and to the fact that values can all receive
the empty intersection type, we can conclude that all terminating terms are themselves typable.
The converse inclusion, however, is not a direct consequence of Lemma
4.6

and, as usual, requires an argument of a higher logical complexity, which we are going to describe.

To precisely characterize the properties of
\mathsf{HEBI}
types,
we define a
reducibility set
for each such type.
The reducibility set of a type
\tau
corresponds to
the set of terms that are expected to
behave according
to
\tau
, although not necessarily typable.
For example, the reducibility set of a value type
\mathcal{M}

consists of values that can be used as intended by all types in
\mathcal{M}
, while
the reducibility set of a computation type
\mathcal{E}
includes all
of computations that behave according to
\mathcal{E}
, i.e., that perform the
operations specified in
\mathcal{E}
in such a way that the continuations can receive
the types as from
\mathcal{E}
. This definition is intended to ensure that the behavior of terms is
preserved even when they are encapsulated within an evaluation context,
provided that the context is well-formed.
Reducibility sets of types, contexts and judgements are defined as follows, by induction.

Definition 4.7 (Reducibility Sets).

First, we define for every type
\tau
, for every typing context
\Gamma
and
for every expression in the form
\Gamma\vdash\tau
, the corresponding
reducibility sets
\llbracket\tau\rrbracket
,
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
, and
\llbracket\Gamma\vdash\tau\rrbracket
as follows.

\hskip-28.45274pt\begin{array}[]{rcl}\\
\llbracket\left\{\mathsf{n}\right\}\rrbracket&\coloneqq&\{\underline{n}\}\\
\llbracket\left\{\mathcal{M}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}\rrbracket&\coloneqq&\bigcap\limits_{i\in I}\{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\texttt{Val}}}\;|\;\forall\;{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}\in\llbracket\mathcal{M}_{i}\rrbracket,\;{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}\in\llbracket\mathcal{E}_{i}\rrbracket\}\\[14.22636pt]
\llbracket\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}\rrbracket&\coloneqq&\{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}\;|\;\exists\;{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\in\llbracket\mathcal{M}\rrbracket,\;{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\twoheadrightarrow{}{}\mathsf{return}({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v})\}\\
\llbracket\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I}\rrbracket&\coloneqq&\{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}\;|\;\exists\;{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\in\llbracket\mathcal{M}\rrbracket,\,\exists\;{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}\in\bigcap\limits_{i\in I}\llbracket x:\mathcal{N}_{i}\vdash\mathcal{E}_{i}\rrbracket,\par\\
&&\hskip 142.26378pt{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\twoheadrightarrow{}{}\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};x.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u})\}\\[14.22636pt]
\llbracket\mathcal{E}\Rightarrow\mathcal{F}\rrbracket&\coloneqq&\{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}\texttt{Hdl}}}\;|\;\forall\;{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\in\llbracket\mathcal{E}\rrbracket,\;\mathsf{with}\,{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\,\mathsf{handle}\,{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\in\llbracket\mathcal{F}\rrbracket\}\\[14.22636pt]
\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket&\coloneqq&\{({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}_{i})_{i\in I}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\texttt{Val}}}^{I}\;|\;\forall i\in I,\;{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}_{i}\in\llbracket\mathcal{M}_{i}\rrbracket\}\\
&&\hskip 85.35826pt\text{ where }\Gamma=(x_{i}:\mathcal{M}_{i})_{i\in I}\\
\llbracket\Gamma\vdash\tau\rrbracket&\coloneqq&\{p\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\texttt{Val}}}\cup\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}\cup\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}\texttt{Hdl}}}\;|\;\forall\;\vec{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}}\in\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket,\;p\{\Gamma\backslash\vec{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}}\}\in\llbracket\tau\rrbracket\}\end{array}

Where for
\Gamma=x_{1}:\mathcal{M}_{1}\dots x_{n}:\mathcal{M}_{n}

and
\vec{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}}\in\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket
,

p\{\Gamma\backslash\vec{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}}\}\coloneqq p\{x_{1}\backslash{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}_{1}\dots x_{n}\backslash{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}_{n}\}
.

Some of the inductive cases in
Definition

4.7
are standard. In particular, the treatment of
values is exactly the same as in traditional intersection types, in which the Tait’s reducibility
is well known to work well. Functional values, in particular, are handled by universally quantifying over the elements of the reducibility set of the argument type, requiring that the corresponding application is itself reducible. The treatment of computations and handlers, on the other hand, is novel and deserves some discussion. While the reducibility set of the type
\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}
is defined in a very natural way, that of the type
\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I}

may appear less conventional: for a term to belong to it, it is required not only that the term reduces to one
in the appropriate canonical form, but also that the continuation is itself reducible seen as an open term. It is also noteworthy that the reducibility set
\llbracket\mathcal{E}\Rightarrow\mathcal{F}\rrbracket
, whose elements are handlers, behaves structurally analogously to a genuine function type, where, however, the role of term application is played by the construct
\mathsf{with}
.

The first result on reducibility sets we present concerns type contexts, and states that the operator
+
on type contexts behaves exactly as expected, namely that it gets interpreted as set-theoretic intersection.

Remark 4.8 (Context Reducibility).

If
\vec{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}}\in\llbracket\Gamma+\Gamma^{\prime}\rrbracket

then
\vec{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}}\in\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\cap\llbracket\Gamma^{\prime}\rrbracket
.

Another important step is that reducibility sets are stable under reduction and expansion, that is, that a
saturation
property holds.

Lemma 4.9 (Reducibility Expansion and Reduction).

If
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\rightarrow{}{}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}^{\prime}
then

{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}^{\prime}\in\llbracket\mathcal{E}\rrbracket
iff
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\in\llbracket\mathcal{E}\rrbracket
.

Its proof follows from the fact that reduction is deterministic: for every
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
there is
at most
one term
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\rightarrow{}{}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}
.
The final step establishes the connection between typability and membership in reducibility sets.

Lemma 4.10 (Fundamental Lemma).

If
\Pi\mathrel{\triangleright}\Gamma\vdash p:\tau
then
p\in\llbracket\Gamma\vdash\tau\rrbracket
.

Proof Sketch.

This goes by induction on the structure of
\Pi
. In some inductive cases, we make use of Lemma
4.9
.
∎

This characterization allows us to establish a strong
connection between reachability and
\mathsf{HEBI}
typability.

Theorem 4.11 (
\mathsf{HEBI}
Reachability).

For every closed
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
it holds that

\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\left\{\mathsf{n}\right\}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}

if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\twoheadrightarrow{}{}\mathsf{return}(\underline{n})
.

Proof.

Suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
is a closed term and assume
\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\left\{\mathsf{n}\right\}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}
. From
Lemma

4.10
it follows that
t\in\llbracket\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\left\{\mathsf{n}\right\}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}\rrbracket
, from which, by Definition
4.7
, we deduce that
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\twoheadrightarrow{}{}\mathsf{return}(\underline{n})
. Conversely, if
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\twoheadrightarrow{}{}\mathsf{return}(\underline{n})
, let
m
be the number of reduction steps. By induction on
m
one proves that necessarily
\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\left\{\mathsf{n}\right\}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}
: in the base case this is immediate, while in the inductive step Lemma
4.6
applies.
∎

The type system
\mathsf{HEBI}
characterizes not only reachability in the sense of Theorem
4.11
, but also termination. In this respect, we say that a closed term
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
is terminating if the only reduction sequence
t=t_{1}\rightarrow t_{2}\rightarrow t_{3}\rightarrow\cdots
has finite length. Not only are all terms typable in
\mathsf{HEBI}
terminating, as can be readily deduced from Theorem
4.10
, but the converse also holds.

5.
Behavioral Simple Typing and Model Checking

In the last section, we introduced a notion of behavioral intersection typing for
\mathsf{HE}
terms and proved that it precisely characterizes termination and reachability. In doing so, we observed that intersection types naturally acquire a behavioural interpretation, whereby the operations performed become an integral part of the underlying type.

The price to pay for all that, of course, is that type checking necessarily becomes undecidable, as is to be expected in a system featuring intersection types. At this point, however, it is natural to ask whether dispensing with intersections while retaining the behavioural component yields a system with satisfactory metatheoretic properties. By this we mean that typing must preserved under forward reduction, (see
Theorem

5.1
) and that it must satisfy the same form of progess arleady explained in the case of
\mathsf{HEBI}
(see
Lemma

5.2
). On the other hand, we do not require the system to satisfy subject expansion, as is standard for non-intersection type systems.
In this section, we show that this question admits a positive answer. Specifically, we prove that a system, denoted
\mathsf{HEB}
, obtained from
\mathsf{HEBI}
in the manner outlined above, not only enjoys these desirable properties, but also features a decidable reachability problem.
We establish this decidability result as follows.
First, we define a
refinement relation
between
\mathsf{HEB}
typing derivations and those of
\mathsf{HEBI}
.
This relation allows us to identify the space of
\mathsf{HEBI}
typing derivations accommodating a term that is well typed in
\mathsf{HEB}
, and ultimately suggests a way to explore it.
We then show that the space is finite and, based on it, that the underlying reachability problem is decidable.
This technique is essentially the same as that employed in parts of the literature on HOMC
(
30
;
23
;
10
)
, and it is revisited here by making explicit use of the refinement relation between typing derivations, thereby yielding a more streamlined presentation.

5.1.
The
\mathsf{HEB}
Type System

The grammars defining
\mathsf{HEB}
types are as follows.

\begin{array}[]{crcl}\textbf{(Value Types)}&\mathsf{M},\mathsf{N}&\coloneqq&\mathsf{n}\;|\;\mathsf{M}\rightarrow\mathsf{E}\\
\textbf{(Computation Types)}&\mathsf{E},\mathsf{F},\mathsf{G}&\coloneqq&\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathsf{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}\;|\;\prescript{\sigma[\mathsf{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathsf{N}\rightarrow\mathsf{E}\}\!\!\}\par\\
\textbf{(Handler Types)}&\mathsf{H}&\coloneqq&\mathsf{E}\Rightarrow\mathsf{F}\par\\
\textbf{(General Types)}&\mathsf{T},\mathsf{U}&\coloneqq&\mathsf{M}\;|\;\mathsf{E}\;|\;\mathsf{H}\par\end{array}

As can be readily observed, the
\mathsf{HEB}
types are obtained in a natural way from those of
\mathsf{HEBI}
through a process of uniformization of intersections, while preserving their behavioural structure. In particular,
\lambda
-abstractions in
\mathsf{HEB}
are assigned a
single
arrow type of the form
\mathsf{M}\rightarrow\mathsf{E}
and, similarly, computations that produce the effect
\sigma
are typed as
\prescript{\sigma[\mathsf{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathsf{N}\rightarrow\mathsf{E}\}\!\!\}
. In
\mathsf{HEBI}
, computation types use intersections to describe the type-level tree of branching effects produced by a computation. In contrast,
\mathsf{HEB}
prohibits this mechanism, ensuring that the algebraic operations performed by a well-typed computation in
\mathsf{HEB}
do not branch and can be
linearly
ordered. But beware: this does not mean that handlers make a linear use of the continuation, which can in fact be copied; rather, all the multiple uses of the continuation still receive
the same type
.

The typing rules of the system are presented in Figure
7
, while the new notion of Leaf Replacement—substantially simplified with respect to that of
\mathsf{HEBI}
—are given in Figure
8
. We write
\Delta
for type contexts of
\mathsf{HEB}
.
The resulting system enjoys robust metatheoretic properties.
Type preservation can be established using entirely standard techniques and, in light of the corresponding property for
\mathsf{HEBI}
, this is not surprising. Although it does not follow as a direct consequence of the preservation result for
\mathsf{HEBI}
, it deserves nonetheless to be stated and proved explicitly

Figure 8.
\mathsf{HEB}
, Leaf Replacement

Theorem 5.1 (
\mathsf{HEB}
Subject Reduction).

Suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t},{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
, that
\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}::\mathsf{E}
and that
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\rightarrow{}{}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}
. Then, there exists
\Xi
such that
\Xi\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}::\mathsf{E}
.

We also state a Progress Lemma, similarly to what we have already done for
\mathsf{HEBI}
.

Lemma 5.2 (
\mathsf{HEB}
Progress).

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
be a closed term such that
\Pi\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}::\mathsf{E}
. Then either there exists
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\rightarrow{}{}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}
, or
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
is a normal form computation, that is, either
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}=\mathsf{return}({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v})
or
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}=\sigma({\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v};x.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u})
for some
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\texttt{Val}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}u}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
.

The
\mathsf{HEB}
typing can be regarded as a mechanism for ensuring the absence of type errors in presence of handlers. Unlike the situation for
\mathsf{HEBI}
, however, this verification technique may yield false negatives, namely, it is
in
complete with respect to reachability. For example, each term that terminates but has an handler that uses the continuation in two or more ways which are incompatible type-theoretically, would not be typable in
\mathsf{HEB}
, while it would be in
\mathsf{HEBI}
.. That said, given a term typable in
\mathsf{HEB}
with an integer type, would it be possible to
automatically
determine the value to which it reduces, if any? The remainder of this section is devoted to answering this question in the affirmative. This might seem surprising, given the corresponding negative result for
\HEPCF

(
13
)
, but the positive result in
\mathsf{HEB}
relies on the fact that the
\mathsf{HEB}
type system can still capture the computation trees produced by terms, unlike
\HEPCF
(the class of capturable trees is more restrictive than that in
\mathsf{HEBI}
, though).
A natural question to ask is then whether type checking is itself decidable for
\mathsf{HEB}
. Although we do not study this question in the present work, we conjecture that it is, particularly in a setting where the programmer is required to annotate variables with their types. Although computation types in
\mathsf{HEB}
have an inherently behavioral nature, checking that a handler is compatible with a given computation type does not seems to be too difficult: even when such compatibility may require assigning multiple types to the same handler, the behavioral type gives an explicit bound on the number of distinct typing.

We conclude this section with a brief discussion on the expressivity of
\mathsf{HEB}
. In particular, this system seems well-suited for scenarios in which it is important to track the order in which operations are executed, as well as to assign different types to the same handler depending on the context. For example, consider a program that relies on a polymorphic algebraic operation for printing a value that may be either a string or a numeric type. In such a case,
\mathsf{HEB}
could help capture both the sequencing constraints and the variation in handler behavior through its typing discipline.

5.2.
Behavioural Refinement

This section introduces a refinement relation, which certifies that the
\mathsf{HEBI}
typing provides strictly more fine-grained information about terms that are typable in
\mathsf{HEB}
.
We start by defining a refinement relation
\mathsf{M}\sqsubset\mathcal{M}
on pairs of an
\mathsf{HEB}
type
\mathsf{M}
and an
\mathsf{HEBI}
type
\mathcal{M}
.
This relation identifies the set of
\mathsf{HEBI}
types
\mathcal{M}
at which a term of an
\mathsf{HEB}
type
\mathsf{M}
may be typable.

Definition 5.2 (
\mathsf{HEB}
, Type Refinement).

Let
\mathsf{T}
be an
\mathsf{HEB}
type and
\mathcal{\tau}
an
\mathsf{HEBI}
type. We define the refinement relation between them in
Figure

9
. Let
\Delta
be an
\mathsf{HEB}
type context and
\Gamma
be an
\mathsf{HEBI}
type context. We write
\Delta\sqsubset\Gamma
if, for each
x:\mathcal{M}\in\Gamma
, there exists
x::\mathsf{M}\in\Delta
such that
\mathsf{M}\sqsubset\mathcal{M}
.

It is worth noting that when
\mathsf{M}\sqsubset\mathcal{M}
, the two types
\mathsf{M}
and
\mathcal{M}
have
exactly
the same structure, but they differ in the fact that whenever intersections occur in
\mathcal{M}
,
all
such intersections must refine the corresponding type in
\mathsf{M}
.
We define
\mathtt{Refn}(\mathsf{M})
to be the set of
\mathsf{HEBI}
types
\mathcal{M}
such that
\mathsf{M}\sqsubset\mathcal{M}
.
Notice that
\mathtt{Refn}(\mathsf{m})=\{\left\{\,\right\}\}\cup\{\left\{\mathsf{n}\right\}\mid 0<n\leq m\}
.
This indicates that, for any positive integer
m
and value
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
, if
\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}::\mathsf{m}
holds in
\mathsf{HEB}
,
\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}:\left\{\,\right\}
or
\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}:\left\{\mathsf{n}\right\}
also holds in
\mathsf{HEBI}
for some
n
, and, furthermore, in the latter case,
0<n\leq m
must hold.
The condition
0<n\leq m
allows us to
bound
the number of the
\mathsf{HEBI}
types that can be assigned to
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}
, by
m
.
This enables computing an
\mathsf{HEBI}
typing derivation from an
\mathsf{HEB}
typing derivation (
Proposition

5.6
).

\begin{array}[]{c}\\[-5.69046pt]
\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 16.47223pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{$\mathsf{n}\sqsubset\left\{\,\right\}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\par}\par}}}$}&&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hskip 11.94666pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$0<n\leq m\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\mathsf{m}\sqsubset\left\{\mathsf{n}\right\}$}}}\par}\par}}}$}&&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 47.1173pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\mathsf{M}\sqsubset\mathcal{M}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\left(\mathsf{N}\sqsubset\mathcal{N}_{i}\right)_{i\in I}$}}\hbox{$\left(\mathsf{E}\sqsubset\mathcal{E}_{i}\right)_{i\in I}$}}}}}\hbox{$\prescript{\sigma[\mathsf{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathsf{N}\rightarrow\mathsf{E}\}\!\!\}\sqsubset\prescript{\sigma[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\mathcal{N}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}\}\!\!\}_{i\in I}$}}\par}\par}}}$}\end{array}\\[14.22636pt]
\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 9.23413pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\left(\mathsf{M}\sqsubset\mathcal{M}_{i}\right)_{i\in I}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\left(\mathsf{U}\sqsubset\mathcal{E}_{i}\right)_{i\in I}$}}}}\hbox{$\mathsf{M}\rightarrow\mathsf{U}\sqsubset\left\{\mathcal{M}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}$}}\par}\par}}}$}&&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 73.12125pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\mathsf{M}\sqsubset\mathcal{M}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathsf{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}\sqsubset\prescript{\mathsf{return}[\mathcal{M}]\!}{}{\{}\!\!\{\,\}\!\!\}$}}\par}\par}}}$}&&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 8.68059pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\mathsf{S}\sqsubset\mathcal{E}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\mathsf{U}\sqsubset\mathcal{F}$}}}}\hbox{$\mathsf{S}\Rightarrow\mathsf{U}\sqsubset\mathcal{E}\Rightarrow\mathcal{F}$}}\par}\par}}}$}\par\end{array}\\[14.22636pt]
\end{array}

Figure 9.
\mathsf{HEB}
, Refinement on Types.

We can now extend the refinement relation from types and typing contexts to typing
derivations
, as shown in
Figure

10
:
\Pi\sqsubset\Xi
states that an
\mathsf{HEBI}
derivation
\Xi
refines an
\mathsf{HEB}
derivation
\Pi
. This definition is given inductively on the structure of type derivations:
\Xi
refines
\Pi
if the derivations of their respective premises are themselves refinements of each other, and this must hold at all levels. For integer values, the constraint imposed by the
\mathsf{HEBI}
derivation ensures that the only admissible refinements are those that correspond exactly to the value
\underline{n}
being typed. Moreover, the
\mathtt{(fix)}

rule can be refined by each of the two
\mathsf{HEBI}
tying rules for fixpoints.

\text{if }\Pi_{1}\sqsubset\Xi\text{ and }\Pi_{2}\sqsubset\Xi_{i}\text{ for each }i\in I\\[14.22636pt]

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 39.48732pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\text{$\mathtt{(hdlr_{\sigma})}$ }$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Pi_{1}\mathrel{\triangleright}\Delta;y::\mathsf{M};r::\mathsf{N}\rightarrow\mathsf{G}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}::\mathsf{E}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\begin{matrix}\{\sigma(y;r)\mapsto{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\}\mathrel{\#}{\color[rgb]{0.6992,0,1}h}\\

\end{array}

Figure 10.
\mathsf{HEB}
refinement for rules, excerpt.

5.3.
Behavioural Refinement Reduction and Expansion

The refinement relation between type derivations we have just introduced was defined in order to restrict the search space of
\mathsf{HEBI}
derivations whenever the term under consideration is typable in
\mathsf{HEB}
. But is all this correct? In this section, we will prove that this is indeed the case.

Suppose we are dealing with a closed term
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
typable via the
\mathsf{HEB}
type derivation
\Pi
with an integer type
\mathsf{n}
, and suppose that we wish to study to which specific value
m\in[1,n]
, if any, such a term reduces. Each such possible value can be seen as a judgement
J
of
\mathsf{HEBI}
refining the conclusion of
\Pi
. Thus, in the spirit of Theorem
4.11
, we might attempt to construct a type derivation for the underlying term having conclusion
J
. However, the search space remains too large if we somehow forget that
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
is typable in
\mathsf{HEB}
. We may instead restrict ourselves to searching for one such derivation
\Xi

refining

\Pi
. If such a derivation
\Xi
existed, the property in question would certainly hold, thanks to Theorem
4.11
. Is it also the case, however, that whenever the reachability property is satisfied, the search can be restricted to
\mathsf{HEBI}
derivations refining
\Pi
? In this section we prove this property, by a relative form of subject expansion property.

It is convenient, on the
\mathsf{HEB}
side, to lift the reduction relation
\mapsto{}{}
from terms to typing derivations. We give here only a brief intuition of the construction.
The goal is to mimick the transformation induced by
Theorem

5.1
on a derivation
\Pi
typing a computation term
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
.
We start by defining a notion of substitution directly on type derivations.
Let
\Pi\mathrel{\triangleright}\Delta;x::\mathsf{M}\vdash p::\mathsf{T}
and
\Sigma\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}::\mathsf{M}
be two
\mathsf{HEB}
derivations and
{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\texttt{Val}}}
a closed term. We define a derivation
\Pi\{x\backslash\Sigma\}\mathrel{\triangleright}\Delta\vdash p\{x\backslash{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\}::\mathsf{T}
by induction on
\Pi
. Intuitively, this is the derivation obtained by replacing each instance of a
\mathtt{(var)}

rule involving
x
in
\Pi
with the entire derivation
\Sigma
, mirroring the term substitution
p\{x\backslash{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\}
at the level of derivations.

More formally, we have that:

\Bigl(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 41.27081pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\text{$\mathtt{(var)}$ }$}}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{$\Pi\mathrel{\triangleright}\Delta;x::\mathsf{M}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}x::\mathsf{M}$}}\par}\par}}}$}\Bigl)\{x\backslash\Sigma\}=\Pi^{s}

Where
\Pi^{s}
is obtained from
\Sigma
thanks to the admissibility of weakening in the system. In the inductive cases, the substitution is propagated to the premises of the rule,
e.g.

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\text{$\mathtt{(abs)}$ }$}}}\hbox{\hskip 8.9826pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Pi_{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\mathrel{\triangleright}\Delta;x::\mathsf{M};y::\mathsf{N}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}::\mathsf{E}$}}\hbox{$\Delta;x::\mathsf{M}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\lambda y.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}::\mathsf{N}\rightarrow\mathsf{E}$}}}\par}\par}}}$}\{x\backslash\Sigma\}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\text{$\mathtt{(abs)}$ }$}}}\hbox{\hskip 26.71521pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Pi_{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\{x\backslash\Pi^{\prime}\}\mathrel{\triangleright}\Delta;y::\mathsf{N}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\lambda \begin{array}[]{ccc}\\[-5.69046pt]
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hskip 8.90016pt\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower \\
\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 3.74632pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 4.44441pt}}(\mathsf{fix}\,x.{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}){\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}::\mathsf{U}$}}\par}\par}}}$}&\rightsquigarrow&\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\enskip\hbox{\lower 2.15277pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle\text{$\mathtt{(app)}$ }$}}}\hbox{\hskip 35.7053pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\begin{matrix}{\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}::\mathsf{M}}\\
{\Pi_{p}\{x\backslash\Pi_{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\}\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\{x\backslash\mathsf{fix}\,x.{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\}::\mathsf{M}\rightarrow\mathsf{U}}\end{matrix}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\{x\backslash\mathsf{fix}\,x.{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}::\mathsf{U}$}}}\par}\par}}}$}\\[22.76228pt]
\end{array}\par

Figure 11.
Example of reduction between two
\mathsf{HEB}
derivations.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
be a closed term and
\Pi\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}::\mathsf{E}
an
\mathsf{HEB}
derivation. We can now define a reduction on derivations, written
\Pi\rightsquigarrow\Pi^{\prime}
, by minimicking the rules in
Figure

1
. Two examples are given in
Figure

11
.

Lemma 5.3 (Relative Subject Expansion).

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t},{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}\in\Lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\texttt{Cmp}}}
be two closed term such that

{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}\rightarrow{}{}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}
. Let

\Xi^{\prime}\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}:\mathcal{E}
and
\Pi^{\prime}\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}::\mathsf{E}
be such that
\Pi^{\prime}\sqsubset\Xi^{\prime}
and
\Pi\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}::\mathsf{E}
be such that
\Pi\rightsquigarrow\Pi^{\prime}
,
then there exists

\Xi\mathrel{\triangleright}\emptyset\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\mathcal{E}
such that
\Pi\sqsubset\Xi
.

The proof of Lemma
5.3
goes by cases on
\Pi\rightsquigarrow\Pi^{\prime}
, and exploits some auxiliary results along the way. But why is reduction on derivations actually needed? In
Lemma

5.3
we have two derivations
\Pi^{\prime}
and
\Xi^{\prime}
typing
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}s}
such that
\Pi^{\prime}\sqsubset\Xi^{\prime}
. In general, subject expansion fails for
\mathsf{HEB}
, therefore we have to suppose the existence of a derivation
\Pi
typing
{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}
. Our goal now is to construct a refinement
\Xi
of
\Pi
. However,
\Pi
need not bear any relationship with
\Pi^{\prime}
and may be completely different from it. The condition
\Pi\rightsquigarrow\Pi^{\prime}
ensures that
\Pi
is suitably close to
\Pi^{\prime}
, allowing the construction of a derivation
\Xi
from
\Xi^{\prime}
and such that
\Pi\sqsubset\Xi
. By the way, a similar approach has already been used in
(
16
)
to prove a form of Relative Subject Expansion.

5.4.
Finite Refinements and Decidability

Thanks to the results obtained in the previous subsection, and in particular to Relative Subject Expansion, we can say that restricting the search for an
\mathsf{HEBI}
derivation to those derivations that refine a given
\mathsf{HEB}
derivation does not result in any loss of generality. However, it is not immediate that this can be done
automatically
. This is indeed the case, and the key observation from which to start to show it is that every
\mathsf{HEB}
type, by construction, admits a finite number of refinements in
\mathsf{HEBI}
:

Lemma 5.4 (Finite Refinement Property).

Let
\mathsf{T}
be an
\mathsf{HEB}
type. There exists a finite number of
\mathsf{HEBI}
types
\tau
such that
\mathsf{T}\sqsubset\tau
.

This is an easy induction on the structure of
\mathsf{T}
. Noticeably, in the inductive cases one exploits the fact that the powerset of a finite set is finite. As a consequence, the number of refinements of
\mathsf{T}
can be exponential on the size of
\mathsf{T}
.

The existence of a refinement with conclusion
J
for a given
\mathsf{HEB}
derivation
\Pi
can be expressed by means of a logical formula
\mathcal{A}(\Pi,J)
, defined by induction on
\Pi
, some of whose defining clauses are given in Figure
12
.

\begin{array}[]{ccl}\\[-5.69046pt]
\mathcal{A}\Biggl(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hskip 9.53003pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$0<n\leq m\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\Delta\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\underline{n}::\mathsf{m}$}}}\par}\par}}}$},\,\Gamma\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\underline{n}:\mathcal{M}\in\left\{\left\{\,\right\},\left\{\mathsf{i}\right\}\right\}\Biggl)&=&(\Gamma=\emptyset)\wedge((\mathcal{M}=\emptyset)\lor(i=n))\\[14.22636pt]
\mathcal{A}\Biggl(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 37.79857pt\hbox{}}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{}}\hbox{$\Delta;x::\mathsf{M}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}x::\mathsf{M}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\par}\par}}}$},\,\Gamma;x:\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}x:\mathcal{M}\Biggl)&=&(\Gamma=\emptyset)\land\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\land\mathsf{M}\sqsubset\mathcal{M}\\[14.22636pt]
\mathcal{A}\Biggl(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hskip 12.79161pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Pi^{\prime}\mathrel{\triangleright}\Delta;x::\mathsf{M}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}::\mathsf{E}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\Delta\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\lambda x.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}::\mathsf{M}\rightarrow\mathsf{E}$}}}\par}\par}}}$},\,\Gamma\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\lambda x.{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}t}:\{\mathcal{M}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i}\}_{i\in I}\Biggl)&=&\begin{array}[]{c}\forall i\in I.\,\exists\Gamma_{i}\subseteq\Gamma.\,(\Gamma=+_{i\in I}\Gamma_{i})\\

\end{array}\\[14.22636pt]
\mathcal{A}\Biggl(\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hskip 22.20834pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Pi^{\prime}\mathrel{\triangleright}\Delta;x::\mathsf{M}\rightarrow\mathsf{E}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}::\mathsf{M}\rightarrow\mathsf{E}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\Delta\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\mathsf{fix}\,x.{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}::\mathsf{M}\rightarrow\mathsf{E}$}}}\par}\par}}}$},\\[8.5359pt]
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\Gamma\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}\mathsf{fix}\,x.{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}:\mathcal{N}\end{array}\Biggl)&=&\begin{array}[]{c}\exists n\leq|\mathtt{Refn}(\mathsf{M}\rightarrow\mathsf{E})|\cdot|Sub(\Gamma)|.\\

\bigwedge_{j\leq n}\mathcal{A}(\Pi^{\prime},\Gamma_{j};x:\mathcal{M}_{j}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}:\mathcal{M}_{j+1})\\
\end{array}\\[28.45274pt]
\mathcal{A}\Biggl(\begin{array}[]{c}\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{}\vbox{\hbox{\lower 3.21385pt\vbox{\vskip 3.01385pt\hrule height=0.4pt\vskip 3.01385pt\par}}\hbox{\hskip 20.26512pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox{}\hskip 15.00002pt\hbox{\hbox{\hbox{}\hbox{$\Pi_{2}\mathrel{\triangleright}\Delta\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}::\mathsf{M}\hfil\lx@intercol$}}\hbox{$\Pi_{1}\mathrel{\triangleright}\Delta\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}::\mathsf{M}\rightarrow\mathsf{E}$}}}}\hbox{$\Delta\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.4805,0.7773,0.0977}C}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}::\mathsf{E}$}}}\par}\par}}}$},\\[8.5359pt]
\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\Gamma\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 4.44441pt\hskip-4.44441pt\hbox{${\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}\vdash}$}\hskip-4.44441pt\hskip-1.86803pt\raisebox{7.44444pt}{\hbox{$\mkern 2.0mu{\scriptscriptstyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}V}}}$}}\hskip-1.86803pt\hskip 4.44441pt}}{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}v}\mkern 1.0mu{\color[rgb]{0.9688,0.4766,0}w}:\mathcal{E}\end{array}\Biggl)&=&\begin{array}[]{c}\exists\mathsf{M}\sqsubset\mathcal{M}.\,\exists\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}\subseteq\Gamma.\,(\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{2})\\
